First, a hierarchical knowledge base combines executable skills with object-centric knowledge of affordances, spatial relations, and action effects, learned from embodied data and online task solving. Second, scene grounding connects this knowledge to the current environment by identifying relevant objects and resolving action-critical quantities. Third, multi-stage planning with self-designed experiments checks whether each stage produces its expected physical effects and actively tests competing hypotheses to resolve physical uncertainties. RoboAuto achieves superior performance across five benchmark families, attaining an 89.1% success rate on short-horizon LIBERO-PRO tasks and more than twice the mean success rate of ASPIRE on LIBERO-PRO Long and LIBERO-Plus. | **[Robotic Agentic Platform for Intelligent Electric Vehicle Disassembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18520v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Electric vehicles (EV) create an urgent need for scalable battery recycling, yet disassembly of EV battery packs remains largely manual due to high design variability. | **[Grasp-Then-Plan with Failure Attribution: A Closed Two-Stage Framework for Precise and Generalizable Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03385v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In robotic manipulation, the tight coupling between grasping and motion planning often obscures the true source of failure, leading to inefficient trial-and-error. The swarm holds well: collisions stay low in small swarms, rising steeply as the packing limit approaches, while coordination and cohesion degrade gently rather than collapsing. | **[FlowHOI: Flow-based Semantics-Grounded Generation of Hand-Object Interactions for Dexterous Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13444v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent vision-language-action (VLA) models can generate plausible end-effector motions, yet they often fail in long-horizon, contact-rich tasks because the underlying hand-object interaction (HOI) structure is not explicitly represented. We propose FlowHOI, a two-stage flow-matching framework that generates semantically grounded, temporally coherent HOI sequences, comprising hand poses, object poses, and hand-object contact states, conditioned on an egocentric observation, a language instruction, and a 3D Gaussian splatting (3DGS) scene reconstruction. We decouple geometry-centric grasping from semantics-centric manipulation, conditioning the latter on compact 3D scene tokens and employing a motion-text alignment loss to semantically ground the generated interactions in both the physical scene layout and the language instruction. To address the scarcity of high-fidelity HOI supervision, we introduce a reconstruction pipeline that recovers aligned hand-object trajectories and meshes from large-scale egocentric videos, yielding an HOI prior for robust generation. Across the GRAB and HOT3D benchmarks, FlowHOI achieves the highest action recognition accuracy and a 1.7$\times$ higher physics simulation success rate than the strongest diffusion-based baseline, while delivering a 40$\times$ inference speedup. Project Page: https://huajian-zeng.github.io/projects/flowhoi/</p></details> |
| **[RoKON: Efficient and Adaptive Data-Enabled Predictive Control for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35458v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Data-enabled predictive control has recently emerged as a promising control paradigm, enabling a transparent optimal-control formulation without the need to learn an explicit dynamics model. We propose a damped Gauss-Newton method that enables real-time control by efficiently exploiting the least-squares structure of the control problem. In addition, we introduce a hybrid buffer management strategy that incorporates informative online data while retaining relevant offline data, allowing the predictor to account for changes in the dynamics. We demonstrate the tracking performance and computational suitability of the proposed method in MuJoCo using a Franka Research 3 arm pushing a block across surface, without prior knowledge of its changing friction values.</p></details> | 8 pages, 3 figures |
| **[CognitiveReality: Robot-Agnostic Semantic Gaussian Mapping with an LLM Agent for Immersive Collaborative VR Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31418v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A photorealistic 3D view tells a teleoperator where a robot is, but not what the scene contains, how well each object has been observed, or how to turn pointing and speech into robot action. Speech and controller rays are grounded against persistent scene objects through validated typed tools and operator-confirmed robot actions. In the controlled agent evaluation, the deployed local Qwen3-VL-8B router reaches 81.24\% tool exact match, while merge-aware replay correctly redirects 101 absorbed object identifiers. On robot data CognitiveReality exceeds a Gaussian-plus-SDF baseline by 2-8 dB; pose error through 5-40 s SLAM outages stays within 1-8 cm. Deployed live on two quadrupeds, the agent executed 26 of 30 navigation requests and 20 of 20 re-observation requests, raising object quality by 2-5 dB.</p></details> |  |
| **[MATE-BT: A Behavior-Tree-Based Framework for Hierarchically Decentralized Mission Autonomy in Heterogeneous Multi-Robot Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35429v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-robot teams executing predefined missions must adapt to runtime changes and sustain mission management despite robot failures. This paper proposes MATE-BT (Mission, Allocation, and Task Execution with Behavior Trees), a hierarchically decentralized framework that separates mission execution, allocation, and task execution. An active onboard leader manages mission progression and team-level task assignment, with authority transferable to another leader-capable robot after failure. In the physical experiments, all MATE-BT computation runs onboard. Hardware-in-the-loop resource measurements further support onboard execution on Jetson Orin Nano and Raspberry Pi 5. By updating only the affected layers without full-plan regeneration, MATE-BT maintained a response time of approximately 130 ms with 30 robots and 120 targets. | **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. Although language-based planning and learned manipulation provide complementary capabilities, their integration requires robots to adapt subsequent operations to actual execution outcomes. For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.</p></details> |  |
| **[Thermal-Headroom Routing for Efficient Robot Perception in Extreme Heat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35231v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adaptive robot perception systems must balance detector accuracy, inference latency, and energy consumption, particularly when high ambient temperatures cause processors to thermally throttle. We propose Thermal-Headroom Routing (THR), a lightweight model-selection policy that forecasts processor temperature using a closed-form RC thermal model and excludes detector configurations predicted to cross a throttling threshold. We evaluate THR with four YOLO11 configurations across seven real-world perception traces, eight ambient temperatures, and 20 randomized seeds, comparing it with latency-aware routing, fixed-threshold thermostats, duty caps, and a matched-compute-budget control. At ambient temperatures of 45-55 degrees C and a tight inference deadline, THR eliminates throttling in simulation, reduces deadline misses by 3.9-7.5 percentage points, and reduces energy per on-time frame by approximately 12-13% relative to a latency-belief router. A separate experiment using deadline-paced, nonoverlapping frame execution yields miss-rate reductions of 6.2-9.5 percentage points. A tuned thermostat or duty cap can match THR at a fixed operating point; predictive routing is most useful when ambient conditions or calibrated thermal parameters differ from those used to tune static policies. Measurements on an Apple M5 support the single-node heating approximation but do not validate hardware throttling. The results characterize the conditions under which thermal forecasting improves the efficiency and timeliness of resource-constrained robot perception.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 5 figures, 11 tables. Code and experimental outputs: https://github.com/ImSpxrsh/thermal-headroom-routing {Submitted IEEE Big Data}</p></details> |
| **[Active Perception of Upper-Limb Functional Mobility through Human-Robot Handovers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19896v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The reintegration of people in the workplace after an upper-limb injury is often a long process. Collaborative robots and humanoids operating in shared workspaces must adapt their behaviour to the physical capabilities of their human partners. Achieving this requires robots to autonomously estimate task-relevant human mobility during interaction, without relying on external clinical assessments. In this work, we formulate the estimation of upper-limb functional mobility as an active perception problem. We model task-available upper-limb functional ranges of motion through impairment coefficients that parameterise reductions of healthy joint limits, and integrate this representation into a human model used by the robot during interaction. Based on this formulation, we propose an online active perception pipeline in which the robot actively probes the user through repeated handover requests while progressively updating a Bayesian belief over the impairment coefficients. This enables the robot to continuously refine its internal model of the user's joint-level capabilities through interaction rather than passive observation. The proposed framework was evaluated through simulation studies and a proof-of-concept physical validation with a robotic platform and enacted human impairments, demonstrating that the pipeline can accurately recover the impairment coefficients during real human-robot handovers.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLM-Guided Future Hypotheses for Horizon-Aware Exploration in Multi-Step Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29864v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-step robot manipulation requires acting under uncertainty about how the scene will evolve, making exploration and policy adaptation challenging. We formalize this idea through Future-Experience Conditioning (FEC), a simple interface that conditions closed-loop policies on a latent representation of a short future video. In our simulation setup, future clips are generated in three stages, an LLM reasoner operating over a task ontology initialized from the current scene state, a robot-free digital-twin rollout of the intended object motion, and a mask-free video diffusion model that synthesizes a robot-consistent future clip without requiring segmentation at inference. We instantiate this future-conditioning interface primarily with BC and BC+RL, and compare against a future-conditioned Streaming Flow Policy (SFP) baseline on RoboCasa and CALVIN under NoFuture, GTFuture, GenFuture, and WrongFuture. Generated futures improve performance over no-future conditioning, while mismatched futures degrade it, and our BC+RL instantiation achieves the strongest overall results. An average BC+RL learning-curve analysis across 8 CALVIN tasks further shows that GTFuture improves fastest, GenFuture improves earlier and to a higher level than NoFuture, and WrongFuture remains at zero throughout training. These results suggest that short-horizon future videos can serve as useful structured priors for exploration and policy adaptation under imperfect future predictions. https://enact2026.github.io/</p></details> |  |
| **[Behavioural Quality Is Not Learning Utility: Objective Mismatch in Human Behaviour Models for Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34839v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human behaviour models are increasingly used to provide simulated human interactions for training robots via reinforcement learning (RL). Existing approaches primarily evaluate these models according to how closely their generated behaviours resemble real human data, while their usefulness as training partners is less commonly assessed. This creates a potential objective mismatch: a model that performs well according to behavioural evaluation metrics may not necessarily provide useful training experiences for downstream robot policy learning. Across two human-robot interaction (HRI) domains, highway driving and object handover, we evaluate different types of human behaviour models using common generative-model evaluation metrics. We consider multiple human trajectory representations and compare the resulting evaluations with the downstream performance of RL policies trained using the corresponding models. We find that existing behavioural evaluation metrics do not consistently predict downstream policy performance across domains and trajectory representations, while their evaluations are sensitive to the choice of trajectory representation. We also show that even when the true testing behaviour distribution is known, training against a different distribution may yield better downstream performance than training directly against the true distribution.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://objective-mismatch-hri.github.io/supplementary/</p></details> |
| **[A Shared Control Framework for Mobile Robots with Planning-Level Intention Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08912v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In mobile robot shared control, effectively understanding human motion intention is critical for seamless human-robot collaboration. This paper presents a novel shared control framework featuring planning-level intention prediction. A path replanning algorithm is designed to adjust the robot's desired trajectory according to inferred human intentions. To represent future motion intentions, we introduce the concept of an intention domain, which serves as a constraint for path replanning. The intention-domain prediction and path replanning problems are jointly formulated as a Markov Decision Process and solved through deep reinforcement learning. In addition, a Voronoi-based human trajectory generation algorithm is developed, allowing the model to be trained entirely in simulation without human participation or demonstration data. Extensive simulations and real-world user studies demonstrate that the proposed method significantly reduces operator workload and enhances safety, without compromising task efficiency compared with existing assistive teleoperation approaches.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by IEEE Transactions on Robotics</p></details> |
